                        <section class="pmc-journal-banner text-center line-height-none" aria-label="Journal banner"><img src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/banners/logo-sensors.png" alt="Sensors (Basel, Switzerland) logo" usemap="#pmc-banner-imagemap" width="500" height="75"><map name="pmc-banner-imagemap"><area alt="Link to Sensors (Basel, Switzerland)" title="Link to Sensors (Basel, Switzerland)" shape="default" href="http://www.mdpi.com/journal/sensors" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer"></map></section><article lang="en"><section aria-label="Article citation and metadata"><section class="pmc-layout__citation font-secondary font-xs"><div>
<div class="display-inline-block"><button type="button" class="cursor-pointer text-no-underline bg-transparent border-0 padding-0 text-left margin-0 text-normal text-primary" aria-controls="journal_context_menu">Sensors (Basel)</button></div>. 2024 Jun 21;24(13):4041. doi: <a href="https://doi.org/10.3390/s24134041" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.3390/s24134041</a>
</div>

<hgroup><h1>Research on Path Planning Algorithm of Driverless Ferry Vehicles Combining Improved A* and DWA</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">
<a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Wang%20Z"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id1"><span class="name western">Zhaohong Wang</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id1">
<h3><span class="name western">Zhaohong Wang</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>School of Automobile and Traffic Engineering, Liaoning University of Technology, Jinzhou 121001, China; 221285047@stu.lnut.edu.cn</div>
<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Wang%20Z"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Zhaohong Wang</span></a>
</div>
</div>
<sup>1</sup>, <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Li%20G"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id2"><span class="name western">Gang Li</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id2">
<h3><span class="name western">Gang Li</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>School of Automobile and Traffic Engineering, Liaoning University of Technology, Jinzhou 121001, China; 221285047@stu.lnut.edu.cn</div>
<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Li%20G"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Gang Li</span></a>
</div>
</div>
<sup>1,</sup><sup>*</sup>
</div>
<div class="cg p">Editor: <span class="name western">Andrey V Savkin</span><sup>1</sup>
</div>
<ul class="d-buttons inline-list">
<li><button class="d-button" aria-controls="aip_a" aria-expanded="false">Author information</button></li>
<li><button class="d-button" aria-controls="anp_a" aria-expanded="false">Article notes</button></li>
<li><button class="d-button" aria-controls="clp_a" aria-expanded="false">Copyright and License information</button></li>
</ul>
<div class="d-panels font-secondary-light">
<div id="aip_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div class="p" id="af1-sensors-24-04041">
<sup>1</sup>School of Automobile and Traffic Engineering, Liaoning University of Technology, Jinzhou 121001, China; 221285047@stu.lnut.edu.cn</div>
<div class="author-notes p"><div class="fn" id="c1-sensors-24-04041">
<sup>*</sup><p class="display-inline">Correspondence: <span>qcxyligang@lnut.edu.cn</span></p>
</div></div>
<h4 class="font-secondary">Roles</h4>
<div class="p">
<strong class="contrib"><span class="name western">Andrey V Savkin</span></strong>: <span class="role">Academic Editor</span>
</div>
</div>
<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2024 May 9; Revised 2024 Jun 11; Accepted 2024 Jun 18; Collection date 2024 Jul.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div>© 2024 by the authors.</div>
<p>Licensee MDPI, Basel, Switzerland. This article is an open access article distributed under the terms and conditions of the Creative Commons Attribution (CC BY) license (<a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/</a>).</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>
</div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC11244110  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/39000819/" class="usa-link">39000819</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>In view of the fact that the global planning algorithm cannot avoid unknown dynamic and static obstacles and the local planning algorithm easily falls into local optimization in large-scale environments, an improved path planning algorithm based on the integration of A* and DWA is proposed and applied to driverless ferry vehicles. Aiming at the traditional A* algorithm, the vector angle cosine value is introduced to improve the heuristic function to enhance the search direction; the search neighborhood is expanded and optimized to improve the search efficiency; aiming at the problem that there are many turning points in the A* algorithm, a cubic quasi-uniform B-spline curve is used to smooth the path. At the same time, fuzzy control theory is introduced to improve the traditional DWA so that the weight coefficient of the evaluation function can be dynamically adjusted in different environments, effectively avoiding the problem of a local optimal solution. Through the fusion of the improved DWA and the improved A* algorithm, the key nodes in global planning are used as sub-target punctuation to guide the DWA for local planning, so as to ensure that the ferry vehicle avoids obstacles in real time. Simulation results show that the fusion algorithm can avoid unknown dynamic and static obstacles efficiently and in real time on the basis of obtaining the global optimal path. In different environment maps, the effectiveness and adaptability of the fusion algorithm are verified.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> path planning, improved A* algorithm, improved DWA, fuzzy control, fusion algorithm</p></section></section><section id="sec1-sensors-24-04041"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>In recent years, with the rapid development of science and technology and the breakthrough of artificial intelligence technology, driverless cars have become a hot topic in the field of the automobile industry [<a href="#B1-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-sensors-24-04041">1</a>]. Driverless ferry vehicles have also been greatly developed on campuses. On many closed campuses, especially in large campus areas, many schools and enterprises are introducing or developing driverless ferry vehicles one after another [<a href="#B2-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-sensors-24-04041">2</a>]. Although a campus is not as complex as urban traffic conditions, it is still full of complex elements and uncertainties. At the same time, the campus scene puts forward higher requirements for the speed and safety of ferry vehicles [<a href="#B3-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-sensors-24-04041">3</a>]. Therefore, path planning is particularly important in the research and application of driverless ferry vehicles.</p>
<p>At present, the more recognized path planning methods in the industry can be divided into global path planning and local path planning [<a href="#B4-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-sensors-24-04041">4</a>]. Global path planning is used to plan a collision-free path according to the known environment map. Common planning algorithms include the Dijkstra algorithm [<a href="#B5-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-sensors-24-04041">5</a>], the A* algorithm [<a href="#B6-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-sensors-24-04041">6</a>], the RRT* algorithm [<a href="#B7-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-sensors-24-04041">7</a>], the ant colony algorithm [<a href="#B8-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-sensors-24-04041">8</a>], and so on. Local path planning is used to plan a collision-free optimal path from the local starting point to the local target point through the continuous acquisition of surrounding environment information by its own sensors. Common planning algorithms include the artificial potential field method [<a href="#B9-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-sensors-24-04041">9</a>], the TEB algorithm [<a href="#B10-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-sensors-24-04041">10</a>], the DWA [<a href="#B11-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-sensors-24-04041">11</a>], and so on. Both global and local path-planning algorithms have limitations. At present, many scholars have carried out a lot of research work in the field of path planning and improved and integrated the traditional path planning algorithm to improve its performance. For example, Tang et al. proposed to filter the redundant nodes in the A* algorithm and use cubic B-spline curves to optimize broken lines, which reduces the number of nodes and makes the overall path smoother, but it is not suitable for path planning in environments with dynamic obstacles [<a href="#B12-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-sensors-24-04041">12</a>]. Wu et al. integrate the APF algorithm with the RRT* algorithm and introduce the virtual field of APF into the search tree expansion phase of the RRT* algorithm, which improves the convergence rate and search efficiency of the RRT* algorithm, but it is only suitable for a simple experimental environment [<a href="#B13-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-sensors-24-04041">13</a>]. Dai et al. introduced the greedy algorithm into the RRT* algorithm, changed the search range of potential optimal parent nodes into a construction path rather than a node tree, which reduced the number of nodes to be searched, and then integrated the DWA to improve the efficiency of autonomous navigation, but the search mode with fixed parameters will inevitably fall into the local optimal state [<a href="#B14-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-sensors-24-04041">14</a>]. Shang et al. introduced an A* algorithm based on variable step size, which shortens the computing time of the algorithm, but it is only suitable for specific scenarios [<a href="#B15-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-sensors-24-04041">15</a>].</p>
<p>Based on the problems existing in the above research situation, this paper carries out the following work for the path planning algorithm of self-driving ferry vehicles: For the global planning method, in view of the low efficiency of the traditional A* algorithm, there are many turning points in the planned path, the vector angle cosine value is introduced to improve the heuristic function of the A* algorithm, and then the search neighborhood of the algorithm is extended and optimized. Finally, the cubic quasi-uniform B-spline curve is used to smooth the global path. For the local planning method, in order to solve the problem that the weight coefficient of the trajectory evaluation function of the traditional dynamic window method is fixed and the environmental adaptability is poor, fuzzy control theory is introduced to dynamically adjust the weight coefficient of the evaluation function of the traditional DWA. Finally, the two algorithms are fused, which solves the problems of the A* algorithm’s inability to avoid unknown obstacles and the DWA easily falling into local optimization in a large area environment, and the performance of the fusion algorithm is evaluated through simulation experiments.</p></section><section id="sec2-sensors-24-04041"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Rasterization of the Environmental Map</h2>
<p>Considering that the longitudinal height of the road can be ignored in the conventional ferry vehicle path planning experiment, the type of map used in the ferry vehicle’s path planning is an occupied grid map [<a href="#B16-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-sensors-24-04041">16</a>], which has accurate environmental modeling and can be updated in real time. The specific construction method is to rasterize the environment map and replace the environment map with several equal-sized square simulations to obtain a raster map. Converting continuous map data into discrete raster data has the advantages of simplifying data processing, providing convenient coordinate positioning, and adapting to different resolutions.</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li>
<span class="label">(1)</span><p class="display-inline">Raster map binarization</p>
</li></ul>
<p>In this article, the black grid is the known obstacle area, represented by the number “1”; the white grid is the drivable area, represented by the number “0”.</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li>
<span class="label">(2)</span><p class="display-inline">How to mark grid positions</p>
</li></ul>
<p>A Cartesian rectangular coordinate system is established based on the two adjacent sides of the raster map, as shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>. The grid position can be expressed as follows:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD1-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm1" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>j</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>*</mo><mi>l</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>0.5</mn><mi>l</mi><mo>,</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>j</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>*</mo><mi>l</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>0.5</mn><mi>l</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(1)</td>
</tr></table>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-f001"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11244110_sensors-24-04041-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cb44/11244110/b6ef51d0ba3a/sensors-24-04041-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="747" width="775" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/sensors-24-04041-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Raster map and raster location marker map.</p></figcaption></figure><p>In the formula: <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm2" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the horizontal coordinate point; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm3" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>j</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the vertical coordinate point; <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm4" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>l</mi></mrow></math></span> is the single-grid side length.</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li>
<span class="label">(3)</span><p class="display-inline">Grid expansion processing</p>
</li></ul>
<p>In order to ensure the safety of the ferry vehicles when driving, when generating a grid map, the obstacles whose areas do not meet a grid are expanded to fill a grid.</p></section><section id="sec3-sensors-24-04041"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Global Path Planning</h2>
<section id="sec3dot1-sensors-24-04041"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Traditional A* Algorithm</h3>
<p>The A* algorithm is a typical heuristic search algorithm that is based on the Dijkstra algorithm and adds a heuristic function [<a href="#B17-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-sensors-24-04041">17</a>]. In addition to considering the actual cost, it also introduces the estimated cost from the target point to the current point, which is mainly used in static path planning when global environmental information is known. It guides the search and expansion of nodes through the evaluation function. The general expression of the evaluation function is as follows:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD2-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm5" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>G</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>H</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(2)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
In the formula:</p>
<p><span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm6" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the total cost estimate from the starting point to the target point via node <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm7" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math></span>. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm8" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the actual substitution value from the starting point to node <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm9" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math></span>. <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm10" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> denotes the estimated generational value of node <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm11" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math></span> to the target point. The distance cost is generally calculated by Euclidean distance, and the formula is as follows:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD3-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm12" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>G</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mrow><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mrow><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(3)</td>
</tr></table>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD4-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm13" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>=</mo><msqrt><mrow><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow></msqrt></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(4)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>
where <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm14" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the current node coordinates, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm15" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mrow><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>r</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the starting point coordinates, and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm16" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>x</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>y</mi><mrow><mi>g</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>a</mi><mi>l</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the target point coordinates.</p>
<p>The A* algorithm mainly selects the best path by maintaining the nodes in the Open and Close lists [<a href="#B18-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-sensors-24-04041">18</a>]. The specific implementation steps of the traditional A* algorithm are as follows:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li>
<span class="label">(1)</span><p class="display-inline">First, add the starting node S to the Open list, expand the sub-nodes of the 3 × 3 domain around the starting point, put the nodes that do not exist in the Close list and the non-obstacle nodes into the Open list, and delete the starting point from the Open list and put it into the Close list.</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(2)</span><p class="display-inline">Traverse the nodes in the Open list at this time, calculate the value of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm17" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> of each node according to the cost estimation function, and mark the node with the lowest value of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm18" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> as <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm19" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>.</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(3)</span><p class="display-inline">Take the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm20" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> node out of the Open list and add it to the Close list. Determine whether node <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm21" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the target point; if it is the target point, then end the search and jump to step (6); the algorithm runs successfully; if not, perform the following steps:</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(4)</span><p class="display-inline">Search for a neighborhood node <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm22" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math></span> that is reachable by node <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm23" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> and does not exist in the Close list. If <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm24" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math></span> already exists in the Open list, the values of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm25" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> for path 1<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm26" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>S</mi><mo>→</mo><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>→</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> and path 2 <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm27" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>S</mi><mo>→</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> are calculated. If the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm28" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> value of path 1 is less than the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm29" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> value of path 2, it is used as the parent node of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm30" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math></span> and its <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm31" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>G</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> and its <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm32" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>H</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> values are updated. If the value of path 1 is greater than the value of path 2, continue querying other nodes in the Open list. If <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm33" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>n</mi></mrow></math></span> does not exist in the Open list, it is put into the Open list, and the cost is calculated. Record the node with the lowest <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm34" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> value as new <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm35" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span>.</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(5)</span><p class="display-inline">Return to step (3) until the target node is placed in the Close list or the Open list is empty.</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(6)</span><p class="display-inline">Starting from the target point, we continue to backtrack to find the final path of the plan.</p>
</li>
</ul></section><section id="sec3dot2-sensors-24-04041"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Improved A* Algorithm</h3>
<section id="sec3dot2dot1-sensors-24-04041"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.1. Improved Heuristic Function</h4>
<p>In order to reduce the inspection of useless nodes and make the path planning directional, the vector angle cosine value is introduced to improve the heuristic function, and the angle range is limited to 0 to 90 degrees. According to the nature of the cosine function, it monotonously decreases between 0 and 90 degrees. This ensures the monotonous constraint of the heuristic function and makes the selection of extended nodes more directional. The improvement steps are as follows:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none"><li>
<span class="label">(1)</span><p class="display-inline">Build a vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm36" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>S</mi><mi>G</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="true">→</mo></mover></mrow></mrow></math></span> from the start point to the target point and a vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm37" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>A</mi><mi>G</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="true">→</mo></mover></mrow></mrow></math></span> from the extension node of the current node to the end node, as shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>. Calculate the angle cosine <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm38" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>cos</mi><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> between the two vectors, as shown in Formula (5):</p>
</li></ul>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-f002"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11244110_sensors-24-04041-g002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cb44/11244110/dfc49848eac1/sensors-24-04041-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="336" width="704" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/sensors-24-04041-f002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Vector diagram.</p></figcaption></figure><table class="disp-formula p" id="FD5-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm39" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>cos</mi><mi>θ</mi><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mrow><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>A</mi><mi>G</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="true">→</mo></mover><mo>•</mo><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>S</mi><mi>G</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="true">→</mo></mover></mrow><mrow><mfenced close="|" open="|"><mrow><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>A</mi><mi>G</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="true">→</mo></mover></mrow></mfenced><mo>•</mo><mfenced close="|" open="|"><mrow><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>S</mi><mi>G</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="true">→</mo></mover></mrow></mfenced></mrow></mfrac></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(5)</td>
</tr></table>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:none">
<li>
<span class="label">(2)</span><p class="display-inline">Filter out the extended nodes whose cosine value is less than 0.5, make the angle of vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm40" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>S</mi><mi>G</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="true">→</mo></mover></mrow></mrow></math></span> and vector <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm41" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mover accent="true"><mrow><mi>A</mi><mi>G</mi></mrow><mo stretchy="true">→</mo></mover></mrow></mrow></math></span> less than 60 degrees, and ensure that the search path is closer to the destination.</p>
</li>
<li>
<span class="label">(3)</span><p class="display-inline">Use <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm42" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mi>η</mi></mrow></math></span> as the weight and adjust according to the actual size of the map, which is the map unit length multiplied by the resolution. The resolution refers to how many grids the <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm43" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> unit length consists of. The following raster map <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm44" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo> </mo><mi mathvariant="normal">m</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> is a grid, so <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm45" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>η</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>. The constructor is used as an evaluation function, as shown in Formula (6):</p>
</li>
</ul>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD6-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm46" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>H</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>H</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>−</mo><mi>η</mi><mi>cos</mi><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(6)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Because the cosine function is a nonlinear monotone function, it can effectively restrict the search node range of the A* algorithm to a certain range. It can better reduce the number of useless nodes and improve the search efficiency of the A* algorithm.</p>
<p>The new heuristic functions obtained from Formulas (5) and (6) are as follows:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD7-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm47" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>F</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mi>G</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><mi>H</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>−</mo><mi>η</mi><mi>cos</mi><mi>θ</mi></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(7)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>The effectiveness of the improved A* algorithm before and after the heuristic function is verified by simulation; the starting point and the end point are set in the same grid map, and the programs are written to run the improved A* algorithm, respectively. The path-planning results are shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-f003"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11244110_sensors-24-04041-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cb44/11244110/19bd9d8e2c8b/sensors-24-04041-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="304" width="727" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/sensors-24-04041-f003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Comparison of heuristic functions before and after the improved A* algorithm. (<strong>a</strong>) Traditional A*; (<strong>b</strong>) improved A* of the heuristic function.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Statistics of the planning results and a summary of the relevant data of the planning path are shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-t001"><h5 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h5>
<div class="caption p"><p>Planning path result.</p></div>
<div class="tbl-box p" tabindex="0"><table class="content" frame="hsides" rules="groups">
<thead><tr>
<th align="center" valign="middle" style="border-top:solid thin;border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Arithmetic</th>
<th align="center" valign="middle" style="border-top:solid thin;border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Number of Inflection Points</th>
<th align="center" valign="middle" style="border-top:solid thin;border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Path Length/m</th>
<th align="center" valign="middle" style="border-top:solid thin;border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Planning Time/s</th>
</tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td align="center" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Traditional A*</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">9</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">21.98</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" rowspan="1" colspan="1">0.91</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Improve A*</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">7</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">21.77</td>
<td align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">0.87</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/sensors-24-04041-t001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>From the path in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a> and the data in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>, we can see that the A* algorithm with an improved heuristic function has fewer path inflection points and a more directional search, and the path length and planning time have more advantages than the traditional A* algorithm.</p></section><section id="sec3dot2dot2-sensors-24-04041"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.2. Expand the Search Neighborhood</h4>
<p>The traditional A* algorithm needs to traverse the map nodes when planning the path. The more nodes are traversed, the longer the search time is. When the environmental map is large, the traditional A* algorithm will traverse many nodes on the map when planning the path, resulting in a long search time, and the planned path is not smooth enough to meet the planning needs of the ferry vehicles. The number of nodes included in the Close list of the algorithm is proportional to the planning time of the algorithm. The more nodes are included, the longer the planning time is, so the planning time of the algorithm can be reduced by reducing the number of nodes in the Close list. Therefore, this section reduces the number of nodes in the Close list by expanding the search neighborhood.</p>
<p>The traditional A* algorithm searches the grid of the 3 × 3 neighborhood around the current node each time, which is shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-f004"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cb44/11244110/67587a10d269/sensors-24-04041-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="586" width="584" alt="Figure 4"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/sensors-24-04041-f004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Search the neighborhood with 3 × 3.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The traditional 3 × 3 search neighborhood will explore the eight adjacent nodes except the current point each time, select one of the eight adjacent nodes as the optimal node according to the estimated function value, and then repeat the above steps. In this way, the algorithm can only explore eight adjacent nodes at a time, the direction of the planning path is also limited to an integer multiple of <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm48" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>π</mi><mo>/</mo><mn>4</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>, the planning time is longer, and there are more turning points in the planning path. In order to solve this problem, this paper extends the search neighborhood of the traditional algorithm from 3 × 3 to 5 × 5 to reduce the number of nodes included in the Close list, increase the searchable direction of the planned path, and improve the smoothness of the path. The 5 × 5 search neighborhood diagram is shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-f005"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cb44/11244110/57c7acb9b58e/sensors-24-04041-g005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="616" width="612" alt="Figure 5"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/sensors-24-04041-f005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Search the neighborhood with 5 × 5.</p></figcaption></figure><p>As can be seen from <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>, the scope of the 5 × 5 search neighborhood is wider, and the number of adjacent nodes in each search increases from 8 to 24, which is three times higher than that of the traditional A* algorithm, and 1 of every 24 neighboring nodes is included in the Close list. Compared with the traditional algorithm, the number of nodes included in the Close list can be effectively reduced, and the search time of the algorithm will be reduced. At the same time, the searchable direction of each traversal process of the algorithm is also improved; the search direction rises from 8 to 16, the turning angle of the path in the search step becomes smaller, the sharp nodes in the planning path can be reduced, and the smoothness of the path can be improved accordingly. The effectiveness of the 5 × 5 search neighborhood A* algorithm is verified by simulation, and the starting point and end point are set. Based on the improved heuristic function, the A* algorithm is programmed to search the neighborhood with 3 × 3 and 5 × 5, respectively, and the path planning results are shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>. The blue dots in the figure are nodes that have been traversed.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-f006"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11244110_sensors-24-04041-g006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cb44/11244110/507af65c6e57/sensors-24-04041-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="296" width="720" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/sensors-24-04041-f006/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Schematic diagram of the planning result. The lines represent the searched paths and the circles represent the searched nodes. (<strong>a</strong>) Search the neighborhood with 3 × 3; (<strong>b</strong>) search the neighborhood with 5 × 5.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Statistics the planning results and summarize the relevant data of the planning path into <a href="#sensors-24-04041-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/sensors-24-04041-t002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>As can be seen from the paths in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a> and the data in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>, the number of nodes collected in the neighborhood of 3 × 3 search is 31, the length of the planning path is 21.77 m, and the planning time is 0.87 s, while the number of nodes in the 5 × 5 search neighborhood is 29, the length of the planning path is 21.31 m, and the planning time is 0.74 s. It can be seen that the length and time of the planning path in the 5 × 5 search neighborhood are better than those in the 3 × 3 search neighborhood, and the planned path is smoother. Therefore, it can be concluded that the method of expanding the search neighborhood can effectively improve the planning efficiency and path quality of the algorithm.</p>
<p>Because of the large area of the campus scene map and more grids, we can expand the larger search neighborhood to plan the path. In the same way, the A* algorithm based on the improved heuristic function extends the 7 × 7 search neighborhood and the 9 × 9 search neighborhood, respectively, and selects two different maps to verify the algorithm. The search results of different search neighborhoods are shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-f007"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h5>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/sensors-24-04041-t004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>From the planning results in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a> and <a href="#sensors-24-04041-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>, we can see that the path planning results of the algorithm in different search neighborhoods are also quite different. In the two maps, the path length and planning time of the 9 × 9 search neighborhood are the best; there are more grids in the 9 × 9 search neighborhood, so it will reduce the search time of the algorithm, and the overall planning path is smoother. Therefore, this paper uses a 9 × 9 search neighborhood as the scheme to improve the A* algorithm.</p></section><section id="sec3dot2dot3-sensors-24-04041"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2.3. Smoothing Treatment</h4>
<p>After improving the heuristic function and expanding the search neighborhood optimization, the A* algorithm has seen a significant improvement in the planning efficiency and quality of the planned path, but the planned path still has the problem of a large number of right-angle turning times. This turning point does not meet the vehicle kinematics constraints and is not suitable for vehicle trajectory tracking control. Therefore, the cubic quasi-uniform <em>B</em>-spline curve is used to smooth the path planned by the improved A* algorithm [<a href="#B19-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-sensors-24-04041">19</a>].</p>
<p>The mathematical expression of the <em>B</em>-spline curve is as follows:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD8-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm49" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mstyle displaystyle="true"><munderover><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><mi>n</mi></munderover><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><msub><mi>B</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mstyle></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(8)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>In the formula, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm50" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the control point and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm51" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo>{</mo><msub><mi>B</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>}</mo><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>i</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mo>…</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo>}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the <em>k</em>-th <em>B</em>-spline basis function.</p>
<p>The basis function is usually defined by <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm52" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mi>e</mi><mo> </mo><mi>B</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>r</mi><mo>−</mo><mi>C</mi><mi>o</mi><mi>x</mi></mrow></mrow></math></span> recursion [<a href="#B20-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-sensors-24-04041">20</a>]. The principle is to construct a recursive formula, which is constructed by low and high order. The expression is as follows:</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD9-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm53" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>B</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mfenced close="" open="{"><mtable columnalign="left"><mtr><mtd><mfenced close="" open="{"><mtable columnalign="left"><mtr><mtd><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>u</mi><mi>i</mi></msub><mo>≤</mo><mi>u</mi><mo>≤</mo><msub><mi>u</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>o</mi><mi>t</mi><mi>h</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>r</mi></mtd></mtr></mtable></mfenced><mtext>             </mtext><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mfrac><mrow><mi>u</mi><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>u</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>u</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>u</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></mfrac><msub><mi>B</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>+</mo><mfrac><mrow><msub><mi>u</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><mi>u</mi></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>u</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>+</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>u</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></mfrac><msub><mi>B</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>,</mo><mo> </mo><mi>k</mi><mo>≥</mo><mn>2</mn></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mfrac><mn>0</mn><mn>0</mn></mfrac><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mtd></mtr></mtable></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(9)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>In the formula, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm54" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo>{</mo><msub><mi>B</mi><mrow><mi>i</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>}</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> denotes the <em>i</em>-th <em>k</em>-th <em>B</em>-spline basis function, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm55" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>u</mi><mi>i</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> represents the <em>i</em>-th parameter point, and specifies <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm56" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>/</mo><mn>0</mn><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow></mrow></math></span>. It is known from the above formula that the <em>B</em>-spline basis function can be solved by spline degree <em>k</em> and node vector U. In this paper, the cubic quasi-uniform <em>B</em>-spline curve is selected to smooth the planned path, and the control point can be set to triple. The basis function of a cubic quasi-uniform <em>B</em>-spline curve is obtained, as shown in Formula (10).
</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD10-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm57" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mfenced close="" open="{"><mtable columnalign="left"><mtr><mtd><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>6</mn></mfrac><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>−</mo><msup><mi>u</mi><mn>3</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi>u</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>−</mo><mn>3</mn><mi>u</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>6</mn></mfrac><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi>u</mi><mn>3</mn></msup><mo>−</mo><mn>6</mn><msup><mi>u</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mn>4</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>6</mn></mfrac><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mo>−</mo><mn>3</mn><msup><mi>u</mi><mn>3</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mn>6</mn><msup><mi>u</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo>+</mo><mn>3</mn><mi>u</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><msub><mi>b</mi><mn>3</mn></msub><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>6</mn></mfrac><msup><mi>u</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></mtd></mtr></mtable></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(10)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>Therefore, the equation of a cubic quasi-uniform <em>B</em>-spline curve is Equation (11):</p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="FD11-sensors-24-04041"><tr>
<td class="formula"><math id="mm58" display="block" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mrow><mn>0</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>u</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo><mo>=</mo><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mn>6</mn></mfrac><mfenced close="]" open="["><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo> </mo><mi>u</mi><mo> </mo><msup><mi>u</mi><mn>2</mn></msup><mo> </mo><msup><mi>u</mi><mn>3</mn></msup></mrow></mfenced><mfenced close="]" open="["><mtable columnalign="center"><mtr><mtd><mn>1</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>4</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>1</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>0</mn></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mo>−</mo><mn>3</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>0</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>3</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>0</mn></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mn>3</mn></mtd><mtd><mo>−</mo><mn>6</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>3</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>0</mn></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>3</mn></mtd><mtd><mo>−</mo><mn>3</mn></mtd><mtd><mn>1</mn></mtd></mtr></mtable></mfenced><mfenced close="]" open="["><mtable columnalign="left"><mtr><mtd><msub><mi>P</mi><mn>0</mn></msub></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><msub><mi>P</mi><mn>1</mn></msub></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><msub><mi>P</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><msub><mi>P</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></mtd></mtr></mtable></mfenced></mrow></mrow></math></td>
<td class="label">(11)</td>
</tr></table>
<p>In the formula, u is the position parameter and <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm59" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mn>0</mn></msub><mo>−</mo><msub><mi>P</mi><mn>3</mn></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> is the control point.</p>
<p>The cubic quasi-uniform <em>B</em>-spline curve is used to deal with the path planned in <a href="#sec3dot2dot2-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link">Section 3.2.2</a>, <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>, and the result is shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-f008"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 8.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11244110_sensors-24-04041-g008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cb44/11244110/a499ab9676a1/sensors-24-04041-g008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="326" width="764" alt="Figure 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/sensors-24-04041-f008/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Smoothed path. (<strong>a</strong>) Map 1; (<strong>b</strong>) Map 2.</p></figcaption></figure><p>As can be seen from <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>, the curvature of the smooth path is continuous, which can meet the trajectory tracking requirements of the ferry vehicle.</p>
<p>The final improved A* algorithm is used to first introduce the vector angle cosine value into the heuristic function of the algorithm to improve the direction of the algorithm, then expand the search neighborhood to improve the search efficiency of the algorithm, and finally smooth the path through the cubic quasi-uniform <em>B</em>-spline curve, improving path quality.</p></section></section></section><section id="sec4-sensors-24-04041"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Local Path Planning</h2>
<section id="sec4dot1-sensors-24-04041"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1. Traditional DWA</h3>
<p>DWA stands for dynamic window algorithm. On the basis of considering the environmental information, kinematic model, and kinematic performance of the ferry vehicle, the DWA samples several groups of velocity combinations <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm60" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>ω</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> in a simulation period from the allowable linear velocity <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm61" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mrow><mi>min</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mrow><mi>max</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> and angular velocity <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm62" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><msub><mi>ω</mi><mrow><mi>min</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>ω</mi><mrow><mi>max</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>, and simulates the motion trajectories of each group of sampling speed combination <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm63" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>v</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>ω</mi><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span>. Then, according to the trajectory evaluation function, the trajectory evaluation value of each group of velocity combinations is calculated, from which the linear velocity <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm64" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>v</mi><mrow><mi>b</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> and angular velocity <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm65" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>ω</mi><mrow><mi>b</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></mrow></math></span> corresponding to the optimal trajectory are selected, and the obtained velocity combination <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML"><math id="mm66" overflow="linebreak"><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>v</mi><mrow><mi>b</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>ω</mi><mrow><mi>b</mi><mi>e</mi><mi>s</mi><mi>t</mi></mrow></msub><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></math></span> is used as the speed instruction in the current motion cycle. Cycle the above steps until you reach the target point. The dynamic window method can be divided into three parts: motion model, velocity sampling space, and trajectory evaluation function [<a href="#B21-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-sensors-24-04041">21</a>]. The flow chart of the DWA is shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-f009"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cb44/11244110/4318bc8ef297/sensors-24-04041-g009.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1891" width="578" alt="Figure 9"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/sensors-24-04041-f009/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Flow chart of DWA algorithm.</p></figcaption></figure><section id="sec4dot1dot1-sensors-24-04041"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">4.1.1. Motion Model of a Driverless Ferry Vehicle</h4>
<p>First of all, we need to establish a mathematical model to simulate the trajectory information of the driverless ferry vehicle. The kinematic model of the driverless ferry vehicle [<a href="#B22-sensors-24-04041" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-sensors-24-04041">22</a>] is shown in <a href="#sensors-24-04041-f010" class="usa-link">Figure 10</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="sensors-24-04041-f010"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 10.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/cb44/11244110/a4f51e2aaa85/sensors-24-04041-g010.jpg" loading="lazy" height="629" width="764" alt="Figure 10"></p>
